The most common [accent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accent_\(sociolinguistics\)) or [dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dialect) spoken by natives throughout Texas is sometimes referred to as [Texan English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texan_English), itself a sub-variety of a broader category of [American English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_English) known as [Southern American English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_American_English).[^cite_note-239][^cite_note-240] [Creole language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creole_language) is spoken in some parts of East Texas.[^cite_note-241] In some areas of the state—particularly in the large cities—[Western American English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_American_English) and [General American English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_American_English), is increasingly common. [Chicano English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicano_English)—due to a growing Hispanic population—is widespread in South Texas, while [African-American English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_English) is especially notable in historically minority areas of urban Texas.&#x20;

At the 2020 American Community Survey's estimates, 64.9% of the population of Texas spoke only English, while 35.1% spoke a language other than English.[^cite_note-242] Roughly 30% of the total population spoke Spanish. By 2021, approximately 50,546 Texans spoke French or a [French-based creole language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French-based_creole_language). German and other West Germanic languages were spoken by 49,565 residents; Russian, Polish, and other Slavic languages by 37,444; Korean by 31,673; Chinese 86,370; Vietnamese 92,410; Tagalog 40,124; and Arabic by 47,170 Texans.[^cite_note-243]&#x20;

At the census of 2010, 65.8% (14,740,304) of Texas residents age 5 and older spoke only [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language) at home, while 29.2% (6,543,702) spoke [Spanish](26825.md), 0.8 percent (168,886) [Vietnamese](32511.md), and [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language) (which includes [Cantonese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonese) and [Mandarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Chinese)) was spoken by 0.6% (122,921) of the population over five.[^cite_note-MLA_Data-238] Other languages spoken include [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_language) (including [Texas German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_German)) by 0.3% (73,137), [Tagalog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagalog_language) with 0.3% (64,272) speakers, and [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language) (including [Cajun French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cajun_French)) was spoken by 0.3% (55,773) of Texans.[^cite_note-MLA_Data-238] Reportedly, [Cherokee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherokee_language) is the most widely spoken [Native American language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_language) in Texas.[^cite_note-244] In total, 34.2% (7,660,406) of Texas's population aged five and older spoke a language at home other than English as of 2006.[^cite_note-MLA_Data-238]&#x20;

With the coming of Spanish Catholic and American Protestant missionary societies,[^cite_note-246] [American Indian religions and spiritual traditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_religions) dwindled. Since then, colonial and present-day Texas has become a predominantly Christian state, with 75.5% of the population identifying as such according to the [Public Religion Research Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Religion_Research_Institute) in 2020.[^cite_note-American_Values_Atlas-247]&#x20;



[St. Mary's Cathedral Basilica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Mary_Cathedral_Basilica_\(Galveston,_Texas\)) of the [Archdiocese of Galveston-Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Galveston%E2%80%93Houston)

Among its majority Christian populace, the largest Christian denomination as of 2014 has been the [Catholic Church](606848.md), per the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) at 23% of the population, although [Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) collectively constituted 50% of the Christian population in 2014;[^cite_note-Religion_in_America-248] in the 2020 study by the Public Religion Research Institute, the Catholic Church's membership increased to encompassing 28% of the population identifying with a religious or spiritual belief.[^cite_note-American_Values_Atlas-247] At the 2020 [Association of Religion Data Archives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_Religion_Data_Archives) study, there were 5,905,142 Catholics in the state.[^cite_note-U.S._Religion_Census-249] The largest Catholic jurisdictions in Texas are the [Archdiocese of Galveston–Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Galveston%E2%80%93Houston)—the first and oldest [Latin Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Church) diocese in Texas[^cite_note-250]—the dioceses of [Dallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Dallas) and [Fort Worth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Fort_Worth), and the [Archdiocese of San Antonio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_San_Antonio).&#x20;



[First Baptist Church of Dallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Baptist_Church_\(Dallas\))

Being part of the strongly, socially conservative [Bible Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible_Belt),[^cite_note-251] Protestants as a whole declined to 47% of the population in the 2020 study by the Public Religion Research Institute. Predominantly-white [Evangelical Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelicalism) declined to 14% of the Protestant Christian population. [Mainline Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainline_Protestant) in contrast made up 15% of Protestant Texas. Hispanic or Latino American-dominated Protestant churches and [historically Black or African American Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_church) grew to a collective 13% of the Protestant population.&#x20;

Evangelical Protestants were 31% of the population in 2014, and [Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptists) were the largest Evangelical tradition (14%);[^cite_note-Religion_in_America-248] according to the 2014 study, they made up the second-largest Mainline Protestant group behind [Methodists](20119.md) (4%). [Nondenominational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nondenominational_Christianity) and interdenominational Protestant Christians were the second largest Evangelical group (7%) followed by [Pentecostals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostalism) (4%). The largest Evangelical Baptists in the state were the [Southern Baptist Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Baptist_Convention) (9%) and independent Baptists (3%). The [Assemblies of God USA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assemblies_of_God_USA) was the largest Evangelical Pentecostal denomination in 2014. Among [Mainline Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainline_Protestants), the [United Methodist Church](32304.md) was the largest denomination (4%) and the [American Baptist Churches USA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Baptist_Churches_USA) comprised the second-largest Mainline Protestant group (2%).&#x20;

According to the Pew Research Center in 2014, the state's largest historically African American Christian denominations were the [National Baptist Convention (USA)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Baptist_Convention,_USA,_Inc.) and the [Church of God in Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_God_in_Christ). Black Methodists and other Christians made up less than 1 percent each of the Christian demographic. Other Christians made up 1 percent of the total Christian population, and the [Eastern](10186.md) and [Oriental Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Orthodox_Churches) formed less than 1 percent of the statewide Christian populace. The [Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints_in_Texas) is the largest [nontrinitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nontrinitarianism) Christian group in Texas alongside the [Jehovah's Witnesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah%27s_Witnesses).[^cite_note-Religion_in_America-248]&#x20;

Among its Protestant population, the Association of Religion Data Archives in 2020 determined Southern Baptists numbered 3,319,962; non-denominational Protestants 2,405,786 (including [Christian Churches and Churches of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_churches_and_churches_of_Christ), and the [Churches of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Churches_of_Christ) altogether numbering 2,758,353); and United Methodists 938,399 as the most numerous Protestant groups in the state.[^cite_note-U.S._Religion_Census-249] Baptists altogether (Southern Baptists, [American Baptist Associates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Baptist_Association), American Baptists, [Full Gospel Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_Gospel_Baptist_Church_Fellowship), [General Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Baptists), [Free Will Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Will_Baptist), National Baptists, [National Baptists of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Baptist_Convention_of_America_International,_Inc.), [National Missionary Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Missionary_Baptist_Convention_of_America), [National Primitive Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Primitive_Baptist_Convention_of_the_U.S.A.), and [Progressive National Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_National_Baptist_Convention)) numbered 3,837,306; Methodists within United Methodism, the [AME](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Methodist_Episcopal_Church), [AME Zion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Methodist_Episcopal_Zion_Church), [CME](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Methodist_Episcopal_Church), and the [Free Methodist Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Methodist_Church) numbered 1,026,453 Texans.&#x20;

The same study tabulated 425,038 Pentecostals spread among the Assemblies of God, [Church of God (Cleveland)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_God_\(Cleveland,_Tennessee\)), and Church of God in Christ. Nontrinitarian or [Oneness Pentecostals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oneness_Pentecostalism) numbered 7,042 between [Bible Way Church of Our Lord Jesus Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible_Way_Church_of_Our_Lord_Jesus_Christ), [COOLJC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Our_Lord_Jesus_Christ_of_the_Apostolic_Faith), and the [Pentecostal Assemblies of the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostal_Assemblies_of_the_World). Other Christians, including the Eastern and Oriental Orthodox, numbered 55,329 altogether, and Episcopalians numbered 134,318, although the [Anglican Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican_Catholic_Church), [Anglican Church in America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican_Church_in_America), [Anglican Church in North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican_Church_in_North_America), [Anglican Province of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican_Province_of_America), and [Holy Catholic Church Anglican Rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Catholic_Church_\(Anglican_Rite\)) had a collective presence in 114 churches.[^cite_note-U.S._Religion_Census-249]&#x20;

Non-Christian faiths accounted for 4% of the religious population in 2014, and 5% in 2020 per the Pew Research Center and Public Religion Research Institute.[^cite_note-Religion_in_America-248][^cite_note-American_Values_Atlas-247] Adherents of many other religions reside predominantly in the urban centers of Texas. Judaism, Islam, and Buddhism were tied as the second largest religion as of 2014 and 2020. In 2014, 18% of the state's population were religiously unaffiliated. Of the unaffiliated in 2014, an estimated 2% were [atheists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheism) and 3% [agnostic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agnosticism); in 2020, the Public Religion Research Institute noted the largest non-Christian groups were the [irreligious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiritual_but_not_religious) (20%), [Judaism](15624.md) (1%), [Islam](6037917.md) (1%), [Buddhism](3267529.md) (1%) and [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism), and other religions at less than 1 percent each.&#x20;

In 1990, the Islamic population was about 140,000 with more recent figures putting the current number of Muslims between 350,000 and 400,000 as of 2012.[^cite_note-252] The Association of Religion Data Archives estimated there were 313,209 Muslims as of 2020.[^cite_note-U.S._Religion_Census-249] Texas is the fifth-largest [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim)-populated state as of 2014.[^cite_note-253] The Jewish population was around 128,000 in 2008.[^cite_note-254] In 2020, the Jewish population grew to over 176,000.[^cite_note-255] According to ARDA's 2020 study, there were 43 [Chabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chabad) synagogues; 17,513 [Conservative Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Jews); 8,110 [Orthodox Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Jews); and 31,378 [Reform Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Judaism). Around 146,000 adherents of religions such as [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism) and [Sikhism](27964.md) lived in Texas as of 2004.[^cite_note-256] By 2020, there were 112,153 Hindus and 20 Sikh gurdwaras; 60,882 Texans adhered to [Buddhism](3267529.md).&#x20;

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Texas)

See also: [Texas locations by per capita income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_locations_by_per_capita_income) and [Texas Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Stock_Exchange)



A geomap depicting income by county as of 2014



Texas counties by GDP (2021)

As of 2025, Texas had a [gross state product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_state_product) (GSP) of \$2.904 trillion, the [second highest of any U.S. state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_by_GDP_\(nominal\)).[^cite_note-2025Economy-257] In 2025, Texas's per capita personal income was \$72,364.[^cite_note-2025Economy-257] Its GSP is greater than the GDP of [Brazil](3634.md), the world's [8th-largest economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)).[^cite_note-258] The [poverty rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_the_United_States) is 14.2%, making Texas the state with 14th highest poverty rate (compared to 13.15% nationally). Texas's economy is the [second-largest of any country subdivision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_country_subdivisions_by_GDP_over_200_billion_USD) globally, behind [California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_California). According to *Business Observer*, Texas ranked third among U.S. states for business formations in 2025, with 457,373 new businesses formed within the state that year.[^cite_note-259]&#x20;

Texas's large population, natural resources, cities, and centers of higher education have contributed to a large and diverse economy. Since oil was discovered, the state's economy has reflected the state of the [petroleum industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Texas#Petroleum). In recent times, urban centers of the state have increased in size, containing two-thirds of the population in 2005. The state's economic growth has led to [urban sprawl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_sprawl) and its associated symptoms.[^cite_note-260]&#x20;

As of May 2025, the unemployment rate was 4.1%.[^cite_note-\:1-261]&#x20;

In 2010, *Site Selection Magazine* ranked Texas as the most business-friendly state, in part because of the state's three-billion-dollar [Texas Enterprise Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Enterprise_Fund).[^cite_note-262] As of 2024, it has the [second-highest number](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_by_number_of_Fortune_500_companies) (52) of [*Fortune* 500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortune_500) companies headquartered in the United States.[^cite_note-\:0-263] In 2010, there were 346,000 millionaires in Texas, the second-largest population of millionaires in the nation.[^cite_note-264][^cite_note-265] In 2018, the number of [millionaire households](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_by_the_number_of_millionaire_households) increased to 566,578.[^cite_note-266]&#x20;

Small businesses, or businesses that employ fewer than 500 people, make up 99.8% of businesses in Texas and employ approximately 5.1 million people, as of December 2025. The number of small businesses increased by 24% from 2017 and 2022.[^cite_note-267]&#x20;

### Taxation

Texas has a reputation for low taxes.[^cite_note-TPoliticalCulture1-268] According to the [Tax Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_Foundation), Texans' state and local tax burdens are seventh-lowest nationally; state and local taxes cost \$3,580 per capita, or 8.4 percent of resident incomes.[^cite_note-TaxFound-269] Texas is one of seven states that lack a [state income tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_income_tax).[^cite_note-TaxFound-269][^cite_note-270]&#x20;

Instead, the state collects revenue from [property taxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Property_tax) (though these are collected at the county, city, and school district level; Texas has a state constitutional prohibition against a state property tax) and [sales taxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sales_tax). The state sales tax rate is 6.25 percent,[^cite_note-TaxFound-269][^cite_note-271] but local taxing jurisdictions (cities, counties, special purpose districts, and transit authorities) may also impose sales and use tax up to 2 percent for a total maximum combined rate of 8.25 percent.[^cite_note-272] Gasoline tax, as of 2025, is 38.40 cents per gallon.[^cite_note-\:1-261]&#x20;

Texas receives more federal spending than it pays in federal taxes, receiving \$1.21 for each dollar paid to the federal government.[^cite_note-273] To attract business, Texas has incentive programs worth \$19 billion per year (2012); more than any other U.S. state.[^cite_note-274][^cite_note-275]&#x20;

### Agriculture and mining



Cotton modules after harvest in West Texas



[Texas longhorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_longhorn) cattle in [Boerne, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boerne,_Texas)

Texas has the most farms and the highest acreage in the United States. The state is ranked No. 1 for revenue generated from total livestock and livestock products. It is ranked No. 2 for total agricultural revenue, behind California.[^cite_note-netstateecon-276] As of 2021, Texas had 126 million acres of farmland, which is the most out of all U.S. states.[^cite_note-\:2-277] At \$7.4 billion or 56.7 percent of Texas's annual agricultural cash receipts, beef cattle production represents the largest single segment of Texas agriculture. This is followed by cotton at \$1.9 billion (14.6 percent), greenhouse/nursery at \$1.5 billion (11.4 percent), [broiler chickens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broiler_chicken) at \$1.3 billion (10 percent), and dairy products at \$947 million (7.3 percent).[^cite_note-EMMA2013-278]&#x20;

Texas leads the nation in the production of cattle, horses, sheep, goats, wool, mohair and hay.[^cite_note-EMMA2013-278] The state also leads the nation in production of cotton which is the number one crop grown in the state in terms of value.[^cite_note-netstateecon-276][^cite_note-279][^cite_note-280] The state grows significant amounts of cereal crops and produce.[^cite_note-netstateecon-276] Texas's fishing industry, along the [Texas Gulf Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Gulf_Coast), is vital to Texas's economy. In 2015, there were 80.4 million pounds of seafood landed, valued at more than \$173.4 million.[^cite_note-281] With mineral resources, Texas leads in creating cement, crushed stone, lime, salt, sand and gravel.[^cite_note-netstateecon-276] Texas throughout the 21st century has been [heavily impacted by drought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drought_in_the_United_States), costing the state billions of dollars in livestock and crops.[^cite_note-282] Farmland has been reduced by non-agricultural development, which includes the construction of data centers. 1.8 million acres of farmland was converted between 2017 and 2022, according to *[The Texas Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Texas_Tribune)*.[^cite_note-\:2-277]&#x20;

### Energy

Main article: [Energy in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Texas)

See also: [Deregulation of the Texas electricity market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deregulation_of_the_Texas_electricity_market)



An oil well



[Brazos Wind Farm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazos_Wind_Farm)

Ever since the discovery of oil at [Spindletop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spindletop), energy has been a dominant force politically and economically within the state.[^cite_note-283] If Texas were its own country it would be the sixth-largest oil producer in the world according to a 2014 study.[^cite_note-284]&#x20;

The [Railroad Commission of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railroad_Commission_of_Texas) regulates the state's [oil and gas industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_industry), gas utilities, pipeline safety, safety in the [liquefied petroleum gas](361384.md) industry, and surface coal and [uranium](31743.md) mining. Until the 1970s, the commission controlled the price of petroleum because of its ability to regulate Texas's oil reserves. The founders of the [Organization of Petroleum Exporting Countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_Petroleum_Exporting_Countries) (OPEC) used the Texas agency as one of their models for petroleum price control.[^cite_note-285]&#x20;

As of January 1, 2021, Texas has [proved recoverable petroleum reserves](63420573.md) of about 15.6 billion barrels (2.48×10<sup>9</sup> m<sup>3</sup>) of [crude oil](23195.md) (44% of the known U.S. reserves) and 9.5 billion barrels (1.51×10<sup>9</sup> m<sup>3</sup>) of [natural gas liquids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural-gas_condensate).[^cite_note-Petrol-286][^cite_note-eia.gov-287] The state's [refineries](195137.md) can process 5.95 million barrels (946,000 m<sup>3</sup>) of oil a day.[^cite_note-Petrol-286][^cite_note-eia.gov-287] The [Port Arthur Refinery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Arthur_Refinery) in Southeast Texas is the largest refinery in the U.S.[^cite_note-Petrol-286] Texas is also a leader in [natural gas](22131.md) production at 28.8 billion cubic feet (820,000,000 m<sup>3</sup>) per day, some 32% of the nation's production.[^cite_note-288] Texas has 102.4 trillion cubic feet (2.90×10<sup>12</sup> m<sup>3</sup>) of gas reserves which is 23% of the nation's gas reserves.[^cite_note-Petrol-286][^cite_note-eia.gov-287] Many [petroleum companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_petroleum_companies) are based in Texas such as: [ConocoPhillips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ConocoPhillips),[^cite_note-289] [EOG Resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EOG_Resources), [ExxonMobil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ExxonMobil),[^cite_note-290] [Halliburton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halliburton),[^cite_note-291] Hilcorp, [Marathon Oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marathon_Oil),[^cite_note-292] [Occidental Petroleum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occidental_Petroleum),[^cite_note-293] [Valero Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valero_Energy),[^cite_note-294] and [Western Refining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Refining).[^cite_note-295]&#x20;

As of 2023, Texans consume, on average, the sixth most energy (of all types) in the nation per capita, following Alaska, Louisiana, North Dakota, Wyoming and West Virginia.[^cite_note-Petrol-286]&#x20;

Unlike the rest of the nation, most of Texas is on its own [alternating current](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternating_current) [power grid](20344155.md), the [Texas Interconnection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Interconnection). Texas has a [deregulated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deregulation_of_the_Texas_electricity_market) electric service. Texas leads the nation in total net electricity production, generating 437,236 MWh in 2014, 89% more MWh than Florida, which ranked second.[^cite_note-296][^cite_note-297]&#x20;

The state is a leader in [renewable energy commercialization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_energy_commercialization); it produces the most [wind power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_power_in_Texas) in the nation.[^cite_note-Petrol-286][^cite_note-298] In 2014, 10.6% of the electricity consumed in Texas came from [wind turbines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_turbine).[^cite_note-299] The [Roscoe Wind Farm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roscoe_Wind_Farm) in [Roscoe, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roscoe,_Texas), is one of the world's largest [wind farms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_farm) with a 781.5 [megawatt](21347693.md) (MW) capacity.[^cite_note-300] The Energy Information Administration states the state's large agriculture and forestry industries could give Texas an enormous amount of [biomass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biomass) for use in biofuels. The state also has the highest [solar power](13690575.md) potential for development in the U.S.[^cite_note-Petrol-286]&#x20;

### Technology



Astronaut training at the [Johnson Space Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnson_Space_Center) in [Houston](13774.md)

With large universities systems coupled with initiatives like the Texas Enterprise Fund and the [Texas Emerging Technology Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Emerging_Technology_Fund), a wide array of different [high tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_tech) industries have developed in Texas. The Austin area is nicknamed the "[Silicon Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon_Hills)" and the north Dallas area the "[Silicon Prairie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon_Prairie)". Many high-tech companies are located in or have their headquarters in Texas (and [Austin in particular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_companies_based_in_Austin,_Texas)), including [Dell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dell), Inc.,[^cite_note-301] [Borland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borland),[^cite_note-302] [Forcepoint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forcepoint),[^cite_note-303] [Indeed.com](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indeed.com),[^cite_note-304] [Texas Instruments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Instruments),[^cite_note-305] [Perot Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perot_Systems),[^cite_note-306] [Rackspace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rackspace) and [AT\&T](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AT%26T).[^cite_note-307][^cite_note-308][^cite_note-309]&#x20;

The [National Aeronautics and Space Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Aeronautics_and_Space_Administration)'s [Lyndon B. Johnson Space Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson_Space_Center) (NASA JSC) is located in Southeast Houston. Both [SpaceX](832774.md) and [Blue Origin](887418.md) have their test facilities in Texas.[^cite_note-310][^cite_note-311] [Fort Worth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Worth,_Texas) hosts both [Lockheed Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin)'s [Aeronautics division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_Aeronautics) and [Bell Helicopter Textron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bell_Helicopter_Textron).[^cite_note-312][^cite_note-313] Lockheed builds the [F-16 Fighting Falcon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-16_Fighting_Falcon), the largest Western fighter program, and its successor, the [F-35 Lightning II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-35_Lightning_II) in Fort Worth.[^cite_note-314]&#x20;

### Commerce

Texas has strong commercial sector consisting of [retail](183515.md), [wholesale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wholesale), banking and insurance, and construction sectors.[^cite_note-Leagueown-16] Texas further has a diverse economy, with multiple *Fortune* 500 companies not based on Texas traditional industries such as [AT\&T](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AT%26T_Inc), [Kimberly-Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kimberly-Clark), [J. C. Penney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._C._Penney), [Whole Foods Market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whole_Foods_Market), and [Tenet Healthcare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenet_Healthcare).[^cite_note-315] Nationally, the Dallas–Fort Worth area, home to the [second shopping mall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highland_Park_Village) in the United States, has the most shopping malls per capita of any American metropolitan statistical area.[^cite_note-316]&#x20;

Mexico, the state's largest trading partner, imports a third of the state's exports because of the [North American Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_Free_Trade_Agreement) (NAFTA). NAFTA has encouraged the formation of [maquiladoras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maquiladoras) on the Texas–Mexico border.[^cite_note-317]&#x20;

### Transportation

Main article: [Transportation in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_in_Texas)



The [High Five Interchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Five_Interchange) in [Dallas](53838.md)

Although the state's large size and rough terrain have historically complicated transportation, Texas has compensated by building the nation's largest highway and railway systems. The [regulatory authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regulatory_authority), the [Texas Department of Transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Department_of_Transportation) (TxDOT), maintains the state's highway system, and regulates aviation and [public transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_transportation) systems.[^cite_note-318][^cite_note-319]&#x20;

Texas is an important [transportation hub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_hub) in America - from the Dallas/Fort Worth area, trucks can reach 93 percent of the nation's population within 48 hours, and 37 percent within 24 hours.[^cite_note-320] Texas has 33 [foreign trade zones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Economic_Zone) (FTZ), the most in the nation.[^cite_note-FTZ1-321] In 2004, a combined total of \$298 billion of goods passed through Texas FTZs.[^cite_note-FTZ1-321]&#x20;

#### Highways

Main article: [Texas state highways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_state_highways)



"Welcome to Texas" sign, entering the state from Arkansas on [Interstate 30](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_30)

The first Texas freeway was the [Gulf Freeway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Freeway) opened in 1948 in Houston.[^cite_note-322] As of 2005, 79,535 miles (127,999 km) of public highway crisscrossed Texas (up from 71,000 miles or 114,000 km in 1984).[^cite_note-323] To fund recent growth in the state highways, Texas has [17 toll roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Toll_Roads_in_the_United_States#Texas) with several additional [tollways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tollway) proposed.[^cite_note-324] In [Central Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Texas), the southern section of the [State Highway 130](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_State_Highway_130) toll road has a speed limit of 85 miles per hour (137 km/h), the highest in the nation.[^cite_note-325] All federal and state highways in Texas are paved.&#x20;

#### Airports

See also: [List of airports in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_Texas)



Terminal D at [Dallas/Fort Worth International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas/Fort_Worth_International_Airport)



Terminal E at [George Bush Intercontinental Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Bush_Intercontinental_Airport) in Houston

Texas has 730 airports, second-most of any state in the nation. Largest in Texas by size and passengers served, [Dallas/Fort Worth International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas/Fort_Worth_International_Airport) (DFW) is the second-largest by area in the United States, and fourth in the world with 18,076 acres (73.15 km<sup>2</sup>).[^cite_note-326] In traffic, DFW airport is the busiest in the state, the fourth busiest in the United States,[^cite_note-327] and sixth worldwide.[^cite_note-328] [Southwest Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Airlines), headquartered in Dallas, has its operations at [Dallas Love Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Love_Field).[^cite_note-329] [American Airlines Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Airlines_Group)'s [American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Airlines) / [American Eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Eagle_Airlines), the world's largest airline in total passengers-miles transported and passenger fleet size,[^cite_note-330] uses DFW as its largest and main [hub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airline_hub).&#x20;

Texas's second-largest air facility is Houston's [George Bush Intercontinental Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Bush_Intercontinental_Airport) (IAH). It serves as the largest hub for [United Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Airlines), the world's third-largest airline, by passenger-miles flown.[^cite_note-331][^cite_note-332] IAH offers service to the most Mexican destinations of any U.S. airport.[^cite_note-333][^cite_note-334] The next five largest airports in the state all serve more than three million passengers annually; they include [Austin-Bergstrom International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austin-Bergstrom_International_Airport), [William P. Hobby Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_P._Hobby_Airport), [San Antonio International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Antonio_International_Airport), [Dallas Love Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Love_Field) and [El Paso International Airport](269621.md). The smallest airport in the state to be designated an international airport is [Del Rio International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Del_Rio_International_Airport).&#x20;

#### Ports

Main article: [List of ports in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ports_in_the_United_States)



[Port of Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Houston) along the [Houston Ship Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Ship_Channel)

Around 1,150 [seaports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seaports) dot Texas's coast with over 1,000 miles (1,600 km) of [channels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_\(geography\)).[^cite_note-ports1-335] Ports employ nearly one-million people and handle an average of 317 million [metric tons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metric_tons).[^cite_note-336] Texas ports connect with the rest of the U.S. Atlantic seaboard with the [Gulf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Intracoastal_Waterway) section of the [Intracoastal Waterway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intracoastal_Waterway).[^cite_note-ports1-335] The [Port of Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Houston) today is the busiest port in the United States in foreign tonnage, second in overall tonnage, and [tenth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_world%27s_busiest_ports_by_cargo_tonnage) worldwide in tonnage.[^cite_note-337] The [Houston Ship Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Ship_Channel) spans 530 feet (160 m) wide by 45 feet (14 m) deep by 50 miles (80 km) long.[^cite_note-338]&#x20;

#### Railroads



[DART rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DART_rail) in Dallas



[METRORail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/METRORail) in Houston

See also: [List of Texas railroads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Texas_railroads)

Part of the state's [tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cowboy#Texas_tradition) of cowboys is derived from the massive [cattle drives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cattle_drives_in_the_United_States) which its ranchers organized in the nineteenth century to [drive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cattle_drives_in_the_United_States) livestock to railroads and markets.&#x20;

The first railroad to operate in Texas was the [Buffalo Bayou, Brazos and Colorado Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buffalo_Bayou,_Brazos_and_Colorado_Railway), opening in August 1853.[^cite_note-339] The first railroad to enter Texas from the north, completed in 1872, was the [Missouri–Kansas–Texas Railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri%E2%80%93Kansas%E2%80%93Texas_Railroad).[^cite_note-340] With increasing railroad access, the ranchers did not have to take their livestock up to the Midwest and shipped beef out from Texas. This caused a decline in the economies of the cow towns.&#x20;

Since 1911, Texas has led the nation in length of railroad miles within the state. Texas railway length peaked in 1932 at 17,078 miles (27,484 km), but declined to 14,006 miles (22,540 km) by 2000. While the [Railroad Commission of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railroad_Commission_of_Texas) originally regulated state railroads, in 2005 the state reassigned these duties to TxDOT.[^cite_note-341]&#x20;

In the Dallas–Fort Worth area, three public transit agencies provide rail service: [Dallas Area Rapid Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Area_Rapid_Transit) (DART), [Denton County Transportation Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denton_County_Transportation_Authority) (DCTA), and [Trinity Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity_Metro). DART began operating the first light rail system in the Southwest United States in 1996.[^cite_note-342] The [Trinity Railway Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity_Railway_Express) (TRE) [commuter rail](6286.md) service, which connects Fort Worth and Dallas, is provided by [Trinity Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity_Metro) and DART.[^cite_note-343] Trinity Metro also operates the [TEXRail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TEXRail) commuter rail line, connecting downtown Fort Worth and Northeast Tarrant County to DFW Airport.[^cite_note-344] The [A-train](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A-train_\(Denton_County\)) commuter rail line, operated by DCTA, acts as an extension of the DART Green line into Denton County.[^cite_note-345] In the Austin area, [Capital Metropolitan Transportation Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_Metropolitan_Transportation_Authority) operates a commuter rail service known as [Capital MetroRail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_MetroRail) to the northwestern suburbs. The [Metropolitan Transit Authority of Harris County, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Transit_Authority_of_Harris_County,_Texas) (METRO) operates light rail lines called [METRORail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/METRORail) in the Houston area.[^cite_note-346]&#x20;

[Amtrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak) provides Texas with limited intercity passenger rail service. Three scheduled routes serve the state: the daily *[Texas Eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Eagle)* (Chicago–San Antonio); the tri-weekly *[Sunset Limited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunset_Limited)* (New Orleans–Los Angeles), with stops in Texas; and the daily *[Heartland Flyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heartland_Flyer)* (Fort Worth–Oklahoma City). Texas may get one of the nation's first [high-speed rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-speed_rail) line. Plans for a privately funded high-speed rail line between Dallas and Houston have been planned by the [Texas Central Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Central_Railway) company.[^cite_note-347]&#x20;

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Texas)

See also: [List of Texas symbols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Texas_symbols)



[The Alamo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alamo_Mission_in_San_Antonio) is one of the most recognized symbols of Texas.

Historically, Texas culture comes from a blend of mostly Southern [(Dixie)](179553.md), Western (frontier), and Southwestern [(Mexican/Anglo fusion)](179571.md) influences, varying in degrees of such from one intrastate region to another. A popular food item, the [breakfast burrito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breakfast_burrito), draws from all three, having a soft flour tortilla wrapped around bacon and scrambled eggs or other hot, cooked fillings. Adding to Texas's traditional culture, established in the 18th and 19th centuries, immigration has made Texas a [melting pot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melting_pot) of cultures from around the world.[^cite_note-348][^cite_note-349]&#x20;

Texas has made a strong mark on national and international pop culture. The entire state is strongly associated with the image of the [cowboy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cowboy) shown in [westerns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westerns) and in [country western music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Country_western_music). The state's numerous oil tycoons are also a popular pop culture topic as seen in the hit TV series *[Dallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_\(TV_series\))*.[^cite_note-350]&#x20;

The internationally known slogan "[Don't Mess with Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don%27t_Mess_with_Texas)" began as an anti-littering [advertisement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advertising_campaign). Since the campaign's inception in 1986, the phrase has become "an identity statement, a declaration of Texas swagger".[^cite_note-351]&#x20;

### Texas self-perception



[Big Tex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Tex) presided over every [Texas State Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Fair_of_Texas) since 1952 until it was destroyed by a fire in 2012. Since then a new Big Tex was created.

"Texas-sized" describes something that is about the size of Texas,[^cite_note-352][^cite_note-353] or something (usually but not always originating from Texas) that is large compared to other objects of its type.[^cite_note-354][^cite_note-355][^cite_note-356] Texas was the largest U.S. state until [Alaska](624.md) became a state in 1959. The phrase "everything is bigger in Texas" has been in regular use since at least 1950.[^cite_note-357]&#x20;

### Arts

Further information: [Music of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Texas)



[ZZ Top](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ZZ_Top) performing in 2016. The [power trio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_trio) are considered cultural icons of Texas and [blues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blues)-inspired [rock music](25423.md).

Houston is one of only five American cities with permanent professional resident companies in all the major performing arts disciplines: the [Houston Grand Opera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Grand_Opera), the [Houston Symphony Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Symphony_Orchestra), the [Houston Ballet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Ballet), and [The Alley Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Alley_Theatre).[^cite_note-houstontheater-358] Known for the vibrancy of its [visual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visual_arts) and [performing arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Performing_arts), the [Houston Theater District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Theater_District) ranks second in the country in the number of theater seats in a concentrated downtown area, with 12,948 seats for live performances and 1,480 movie seats.[^cite_note-houstontheater-358] Founded in 1892, [Modern Art Museum of Fort Worth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Art_Museum_of_Fort_Worth), also called "The Modern", is Texas's oldest art museum. Fort Worth also has the [Kimbell Art Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kimbell_Art_Museum), the [Amon Carter Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amon_Carter_Museum), the [National Cowgirl Museum and Hall of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Cowgirl_Museum_and_Hall_of_Fame), the [Will Rogers Memorial Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Will_Rogers_Memorial_Center), and the [Bass Performance Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bass_Performance_Hall) downtown. The [Arts District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arts_District,_Dallas) of [Downtown Dallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_Dallas) has arts venues such as the [Dallas Museum of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Museum_of_Art), the [Morton H. Meyerson Symphony Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morton_H._Meyerson_Symphony_Center), [the Margot and Bill Winspear Opera House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Margot_and_Bill_Winspear_Opera_House), [the Trammell & Margaret Crow Collection of Asian Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Trammell_%26_Margaret_Crow_Collection_of_Asian_Art), and the [Nasher Sculpture Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasher_Sculpture_Center).[^cite_note-359]&#x20;



[Houston Symphony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Symphony) at the [Jones Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jones_Hall)

The [Deep Ellum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_Ellum) district within Dallas became popular during the 1920s and 1930s as the prime [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz) and [blues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blues) hotspot in the Southern United States. The name Deep Ellum comes from local people pronouncing "Deep Elm" as "Deep Ellum".[^cite_note-360] Artists such as [Blind Lemon Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Lemon_Jefferson), [Robert Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Johnson_\(musician\)), Huddie "[Lead Belly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lead_Belly)" Ledbetter, and [Bessie Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessie_Smith) played in early Deep Ellum clubs.[^cite_note-361]&#x20;

Austin, *[The Live Music Capital of the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Austin)*, boasts "more live music venues per capita than such music hotbeds as Nashville, Memphis, Los Angeles, Las Vegas or New York City".[^cite_note-362] The city's music revolves around the [nightclubs](19321330.md) on [6th Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/6th_Street_\(Austin\)); events like the film, music, and [multimedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multimedia) festival [South by Southwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_by_Southwest); the longest-running concert music program on American television, *[Austin City Limits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austin_City_Limits)*; and the [Austin City Limits Music Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austin_City_Limits_Music_Festival) held in [Zilker Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zilker_Park).[^cite_note-363]&#x20;

Since 1980, San Antonio has evolved into "The [Tejano Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tejano_Music) Capital Of The World".[^cite_note-364] The [Tejano Music Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tejano_Music_Awards) have provided a forum to create greater awareness and appreciation for Tejano music and culture.[^cite_note-365]&#x20;

### Sports

Main article: [Sports in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_in_Texas)

Further information: [List of University Interscholastic League events](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_University_Interscholastic_League_events)



[AT\&T Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AT%26T_Stadium), home of the [Dallas Cowboys](8121.md), who are commonly known as [America's Team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/America%27s_Team)



Playoff game between the [San Antonio Spurs](72889.md) and the [Dallas Mavericks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Mavericks) in [2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_NBA_playoffs); the Spurs won the [NBA Finals](39572289.md) that year.



Texan baseball fans attending a game between the state's two [MLB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MLB) teams ([Texas Rangers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Rangers_\(baseball\)) and [Houston Astros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Astros)) at [Globe Life Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globe_Life_Field) in [Arlington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arlington,_Texas)

Within the ["Big Four" professional leagues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_professional_sports_leagues_in_the_United_States_and_Canada), Texas has two [NFL](21211.md) teams (the [Dallas Cowboys](8121.md) and the [Houston Texans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Texans)), two [MLB](38776.md) teams (the [Houston Astros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Astros) and the [Texas Rangers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Rangers_\(baseball\))),[^cite_note-366][^cite_note-367] three [NBA](22093.md) teams (the [San Antonio Spurs](72889.md), the [Houston Rockets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Rockets), and the [Dallas Mavericks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Mavericks)), and one [NHL](21809.md) team (the [Dallas Stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Stars)). The [Dallas–Fort Worth metroplex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas%E2%80%93Fort_Worth_metroplex) is one of only [twelve American metropolitan areas that host sports teams from all the "Big Four" professional leagues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._cities_with_teams_from_four_major_league_sports). Outside of the "Big Four", Texas also has a [WNBA](57721.md) team (the [Dallas Wings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Wings)), three [Major League Soccer](71802.md) teams ([Austin FC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austin_FC), [Houston Dynamo FC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Dynamo_FC) and [FC Dallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FC_Dallas)), and one [NWSL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Women%27s_Soccer_League) team (the [Houston Dash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Dash)).&#x20;

[Collegiate athletics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_athletics) have deep significance in Texas culture, especially [football](18951490.md). The state has twelve [Division I-FBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Division_I-FBS) schools, the most in the nation. Four of the state's schools claim at least one national championship in football: the Texas Longhorns, the Texas A\&M Aggies, the TCU Horned Frogs, and the SMU Mustangs.[^cite_note-368][^cite_note-369][^cite_note-370][^cite_note-371] According to a survey of Division I-A coaches, the [rivalry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_rivalry) between the [University of Oklahoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Oklahoma) and the University of Texas at Austin, the [Red River Shootout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_River_Shootout), ranks the third-best in the nation.[^cite_note-372] The TCU Horned Frogs and SMU Mustangs also share a rivalry and compete annually in the [Battle for the Iron Skillet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_for_the_Iron_Skillet). A fierce rivalry, the [Lone Star Showdown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lone_Star_Showdown), also exists between the state's two largest universities, Texas A\&M University and the University of Texas at Austin. The athletics portion of the Lone Star Showdown rivalry was paused between 2012, when Texas A\&M left the Big 12 and joined the SEC, and 2024, when Texas joined them.[^cite_note-373]&#x20;

The [University Interscholastic League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_Interscholastic_League) (UIL) organizes most primary and secondary school competitions. Events organized by UIL include contests in athletics (the most popular being [high school football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_school_football)) as well as artistic and academic subjects.[^cite_note-374]&#x20;



[Rodeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodeo) (the state sport) in [Austin](1998.md)

Texans also enjoy [rodeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodeo). The world's first rodeo was hosted in [Pecos, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pecos,_Texas).[^cite_note-375] The annual [Houston Livestock Show and Rodeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Livestock_Show_and_Rodeo) is the largest rodeo in the world. The [Southwestern Exposition and Livestock Show](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwestern_Exposition_and_Livestock_Show) in Fort Worth is the oldest continuously running rodeo incorporating many of the state's most historic traditions into its annual events. Dallas hosts the [State Fair of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Fair_of_Texas) each year at [Fair Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_Park).[^cite_note-376]&#x20;

[Texas Motor Speedway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Motor_Speedway) hosts annual [NASCAR Cup Series](56571945.md) and [IndyCar Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IndyCar_Series) auto races since 1997. Since 2012, Austin's [Circuit of the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circuit_of_the_Americas) plays host to a round of the [Formula 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formula_1) World Championship.[^cite_note-377]&#x20;

The [Panther City Lacrosse Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panther_City_Lacrosse_Club) is a professional lacrosse team in the [National Lacrosse League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Lacrosse_League). They have played local matches at Dickies Arena in Fort Worth, Texas since their inaugural 2021–2022 season.[^cite_note-378]&#x20;

## Education

Main article: [Education in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Texas)

The second [president of the Republic of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Presidents_of_the_Republic_of_Texas), [Mirabeau B. Lamar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirabeau_B._Lamar), is known as the *Father of Texas Education*. During his term, the state set aside three [leagues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_\(unit\)) in each county for public schools. An additional 50 leagues of land set aside for the support of two universities would later become the basis of the state's [Permanent University Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_University_Fund).[^cite_note-379] Lamar's actions set the foundation for a Texas-wide public school system.[^cite_note-380]&#x20;

Texas has [over 1,000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_school_districts_in_Texas) [school districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_district), all administered by the [Texas Education Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Education_Agency), and all districts except the [Stafford Municipal School District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stafford_Municipal_School_District) are independent from municipal government and many cross city boundaries.[^cite_note-381] School districts have the power to [tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation) their residents and to assert [eminent domain](144089.md) over privately owned property. Due to court-mandated equitable school financing, the state has a tax redistribution system called the "[Robin Hood plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robin_Hood_plan)" which transfers property tax revenue from wealthy school districts to poor ones.[^cite_note-382] The TEA has no authority over private or [homeschooling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeschooling) activities.[^cite_note-383]&#x20;

Students in Texas take the [State of Texas Assessments of Academic Readiness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STAAR) (STAAR) in primary and secondary school. STAAR assess students' attainment of [reading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reading_\(activity\)), writing, [mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematics), science, and [social studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_studies) skills required under Texas education standards and the [No Child Left Behind Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No_Child_Left_Behind_Act). The test replaced the [Texas Assessment of Knowledge and Skills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Assessment_of_Knowledge_and_Skills) (TAKS) test in the 2011–2012 school year.[^cite_note-384]&#x20;

Generally prohibited in the [Western world](21208200.md), [school corporal punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_corporal_punishment) is not unusual in the more conservative, rural areas of the state,[^cite_note-385][^cite_note-386][^cite_note-387] with 28,569 public school students [paddled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paddle_\(spanking\)) at least one time,[^cite_note-388] according to government data for the 2011–2012 school year.[^cite_note-CORPUN_US_SCHOOL-389] The rate of school corporal punishment in Texas is surpassed only by [Mississippi](16949861.md), [Alabama](303.md), and [Arkansas](1930.md).[^cite_note-CORPUN_US_SCHOOL-389]&#x20;

### Higher education

Further information: [List of colleges and universities in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_colleges_and_universities_in_Texas)



The [University of Texas at Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Texas_at_Austin)



[University of Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Houston)



[Texas A\&M University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_A%26M_University)



[Rice University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice_University)

The state's two most widely recognized flagship universities are [The University of Texas at Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Texas_at_Austin) and [Texas A\&M University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_A%26M_University), ranked as the 21st[^cite_note-390] and 41st[^cite_note-391] best universities in the nation according to 2020's latest Center for World University Rankings report, respectively. Some observers[^cite_note-392] also include the [University of Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Houston) and [Texas Tech University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Tech_University) as tier one flagships alongside UT Austin and A\&M.[^cite_note-393][^cite_note-394] The [Texas Higher Education Coordinating Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Higher_Education_Coordinating_Board) ranks the state's public universities into three distinct tiers:[^cite_note-UT_System_Tier_One-395]&#x20;

- National Research Universities (Tier 1)

  [^cite_note-396]

  [^cite_note-397]

  - [The University of Texas at Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Texas_at_Austin)

  - [Texas A\&M University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_A%26M_University)

  - [Texas Tech University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Tech_University)

  - [University of Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Houston)

  - [Rice University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice_University)

  - [The University of Texas Health Science Center at Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Texas_Health_Science_Center_at_Houston)

  - [The University of Texas at Arlington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Texas_at_Arlington)

  - [The University of Texas at Dallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Texas_at_Dallas)

  - [The University of North Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_North_Texas)

  - [The University of Texas at El Paso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Texas_at_El_Paso)

- Emerging Research Universities (Tier 2)

  [^cite_note-UT_System_Tier_One-395]

  - [The University of Texas at San Antonio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Texas_at_San_Antonio)

  - [Texas State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_State_University)

- Comprehensive Universities (Tier 3)

  [^cite_note-UT_System_Tier_One-395]

  - All other public universities (25 in total)

The Carnegie Foundation classifies six of Texas's universities as Tier One research institutions:[^cite_note-398]&#x20;

- [The University of Texas Health Science Center at Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Texas_Health_Science_Center_at_Houston)

- [Rice University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice_University)

- [The University of Texas at Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Texas_at_Austin)

- [Texas A\&M University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_A%26M_University)

- [University of Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Houston)

- [Texas Tech University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Tech_University)

Texas's alternative affirmative action plan, [Texas House Bill 588](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_House_Bill_588), guarantees Texas students who graduated in the top 10 percent of their high school class automatic admission to state-funded universities. This does not apply to [The University of Texas at Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Texas_at_Austin), which automatically admits Texas students who graduated in the top 6 percent of their high school class.[^cite_note-399] The bill encourages demographic diversity while attempting to avoid problems stemming from the *[Hopwood v. Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hopwood_v._Texas)* (1996) case.[^cite_note-400]&#x20;

Thirty-six public universities exist in Texas, of which 32 belong to one of the six state university systems.[^cite_note-401][^cite_note-402] Discovery of minerals on [Permanent University Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_University_Fund) land, particularly oil, has helped fund the rapid growth of the state's two largest university systems: the [University of Texas System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Texas_System) and the [Texas A\&M System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_A%26M_University_System). The four other university systems: the [University of Houston System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Houston_System), the [University of North Texas System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_North_Texas_System), the [Texas State System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_State_University_System), and the [Texas Tech System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Tech_University_System) are not funded by the Permanent University Fund.[^cite_note-Smyrl-2020-403]&#x20;

The University of Texas at Austin and Texas A\&M University are the flagship universities of the [University of Texas System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Texas_System) and [Texas A\&M University System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_A%26M_University_System), respectively. Both were established by the Texas Constitution and hold stakes in the Permanent University Fund.[^cite_note-Smyrl-2020-403]&#x20;

The state has sought to expand the number of flagship universities by elevating some of its seven institutions designated as "emerging research universities". The two expected to emerge first are the University of Houston and Texas Tech University, likely in that order according to discussions on the House floor of the 82nd Texas Legislature.[^cite_note-404]&#x20;

The state is home to various private institutions of higher learning—ranging from liberal arts colleges to a nationally recognized top-tier research university. [Rice University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice_University) in Houston is one of the leading teaching and research universities of the United States and is ranked the nation's 17th-best overall university by *U.S. News & World Report*.[^cite_note-405]&#x20;

[Trinity University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity_University_\(Texas\)), a private, primarily undergraduate liberal arts university in San Antonio, has ranked first among universities granting primarily bachelor's and select master's degrees in the Western United States for 20 consecutive years by *U.S. News*.[^cite_note-406] Private universities include [Abilene Christian University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abilene_Christian_University), [Austin College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austin_College), [Baylor University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baylor_University), [University of Mary Hardin–Baylor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Mary_Hardin%E2%80%93Baylor), and [Southwestern University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwestern_University).[^cite_note-407][^cite_note-408][^cite_note-409]&#x20;

Universities in Texas host three presidential libraries: [George Bush Presidential Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Bush_Presidential_Library) at Texas A\&M University,[^cite_note-410] the [Lyndon Baines Johnson Library and Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_Baines_Johnson_Library_and_Museum) at The University of Texas at Austin,[^cite_note-411][^cite_note-412] and the [George W. Bush Presidential Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_W._Bush_Presidential_Library) at [Southern Methodist University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Methodist_University).[^cite_note-413]&#x20;

## Healthcare

Main article: [Healthcare in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Texas)

See also: [List of hospitals in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_hospitals_in_Texas)

Notwithstanding the concentration of elite medical centers in the state, [The Commonwealth Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Commonwealth_Fund) ranks the Texas healthcare system the third worst in the nation.[^cite_note-insurancenet-414] Texas ranks close to last in access to healthcare, quality of care, avoidable hospital spending, and equity.[^cite_note-insurancenet-414] In May 2006, Texas initiated the program "code red" in response to the report the state had 25.1 percent of the population without health insurance, the largest proportion in the nation.[^cite_note-415]&#x20;

The [Trust for America's Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trust_for_America%27s_Health) ranked Texas 15th highest in adult [obesity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obesity): 27.2 percent of the state's population is obese.[^cite_note-416] The 2008 [Men's Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Men%27s_Health_\(magazine\)) obesity survey ranked four Texas cities among the top 25 fattest cities in America: Houston ranked 6th, Dallas 7th, El Paso 8th, and [Arlington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arlington,_Texas) 14th.[^cite_note-obese2-417] Texas had only one city (Austin, ranked 21st) in the top 25 "fittest cities" in America.[^cite_note-obese2-417] The state is ranked forty-second in the percentage of residents who engage in regular exercise according to a 2007 study.[^cite_note-418]&#x20;

Texas has the [highest maternal mortality rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maternal_Healthcare_System_in_Texas) in the developed world, and the rate by which Texas women died from pregnancy-related complications doubled from 2010 to 2014, to 23.8 per 100,000—a rate unmatched in any other U.S. state or economically developed country.[^cite_note-419] In May 2021, the state legislature passed the [Texas Heartbeat Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Heartbeat_Act), which banned [abortion](765.md) from as early as six weeks of pregnancy, except to save the life of the mother. The Act allows private citizens to sue abortion providers and anyone else who assists in an abortion, except for the woman on whom the abortion is performed.[^cite_note-420][^cite_note-421] On August 25, 2022, another law took effect that made committing abortion at any stage of pregnancy a felony punishable by life in prison.[^cite_note-422]&#x20;

Access to [allergy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allergy) and [immunology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immunology) specialists in Texas is unevenly distributed. While the state is served by approximately 462 allergists and immunologists, these specialists are primarily concentrated in major urban centers such as Dallas, Houston, and Austin. In rural areas of Texas, the average availability is significantly lower, with approximately one specialist for every 50,000 residents.[^cite_note-423]&#x20;

### Medical research



The [Texas Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Medical_Center) in Houston

Texas has many elite research medical centers. The state has 15 [medical schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_colleges_and_universities_in_Texas#Health_science),[^cite_note-424] four dental schools,[^cite_note-425] and two [optometry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optometry) schools.[^cite_note-426] Texas has two [Biosafety Level 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biosafety_Level_4) (BSL-4) laboratories: one at [The University of Texas Medical Branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Texas_Medical_Branch) (UTMB) in Galveston,[^cite_note-427] and the other at the [Southwest Foundation for Biomedical Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Foundation_for_Biomedical_Research) in San Antonio—the first privately owned BSL-4 lab in the United States.[^cite_note-428]&#x20;

The [Texas Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Medical_Center) in Houston holds the world's largest concentration of research and healthcare institutions, with over 50 member institutions.[^cite_note-429] Texas Medical Center performs the most heart transplants in the world.[^cite_note-430] The [University of Texas M. D. Anderson Cancer Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Texas_M._D._Anderson_Cancer_Center) in Houston is a highly regarded academic institution that centers around cancer patient care, research, education and prevention.[^cite_note-431]&#x20;

San Antonio's [South Texas Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Texas_Medical_Center) facilities rank sixth in clinical medicine research impact in the United States.[^cite_note-432] The [University of Texas Health Science Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UTHSCSA) is another highly ranked research and educational institution in San Antonio.[^cite_note-433][^cite_note-434]&#x20;

Both the [American Heart Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Heart_Association) and the [University of Texas Southwestern Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Texas_Southwestern_Medical_Center) call Dallas home.[^cite_note-UTSWAboutUs-435] The [institution's medical school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Texas_Southwestern_Medical_School) employs the most medical school [Nobel laureates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nobel_laureates) in the world.[^cite_note-UTSWAboutUs-435][^cite_note-436]&#x20;

## Government and politics

The current [Texas Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Constitution) was adopted in 1876. Like many [states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_constitution_\(United_States\)), it explicitly provides for a separation of powers. The state's Bill of Rights is much larger than its [federal counterpart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Bill_of_Rights), and has provisions unique to Texas.[^cite_note-437]&#x20;

### State government

Main article: [Government of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Texas)

Further information: [Law of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Texas)

See also: [List of Texas state agencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Texas_state_agencies)



The [Texas State Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_State_Capitol) at night

Texas has a plural [executive branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_branch) system limiting the power of the governor, which is a weak executive compared to some other states. Except for the [secretary of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_of_Texas), voters elect executive officers independently; candidates are directly answerable to the public, not the governor.[^cite_note-pluralexec-438] This election system has led to some executive branches split between parties and reduced the ability of the governor to carry out a program. When [Republican](32070.md) president [George W. Bush](3414021.md) served as Texas's governor, the state had a [Democratic](5043544.md) lieutenant governor, [Bob Bullock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Bullock). The executive branch positions consist of the [governor](214883.md), [lieutenant governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lieutenant_Governor_of_Texas), comptroller of public accounts, land commissioner, [attorney general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Attorney_General), agriculture commissioner, the three-member [Texas Railroad Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Railroad_Commission), the State Board of Education, and the secretary of state.[^cite_note-pluralexec-438]&#x20;

The [bicameral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicameralism) [Texas Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Legislature) consists of the [House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_House_of_Representatives), with 150 members, and a [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Senate), with 31 members. The [Speaker of the House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speaker_of_the_Texas_House_of_Representatives) leads the House, and the lieutenant governor, the Senate.[^cite_note-439] The Legislature meets in regular session biennially for just over a hundred days, but the governor can call for special sessions as often as desired (notably, the Legislature cannot call itself into session).[^cite_note-440] The state's [fiscal year](5752099.md) begins September 1.[^cite_note-441]&#x20;

The [judiciary of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judiciary_of_Texas) is among the most complex in the United States, with many layers and overlapping jurisdictions. Texas has two courts of last resort: the [Texas Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Supreme_Court), for civil cases, and the [Texas Court of Criminal Appeals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Court_of_Criminal_Appeals). Except for some municipal benches, partisan elections select judges at all levels of the judiciary; the governor fills vacancies by appointment.[^cite_note-442] Texas is notable for [its use of capital punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_punishment_in_Texas), having led the country in executions since capital punishment was reinstated in the *[Gregg v. Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregg_v._Georgia)* case.[^cite_note-443]&#x20;

The [Texas Ranger Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Ranger_Division) of the [Texas Department of Public Safety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Department_of_Public_Safety) is a [law enforcement agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police) with statewide [jurisdiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurisdiction). Over the years, the Texas Rangers have investigated crimes ranging from [murder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murder_in_Texas_law) to political corruption. They have acted as [riot police](1148542.md) and as detectives, protected the Texas governor, tracked down fugitives, and functioned as a paramilitary force. The Texas Rangers were unofficially created by [Stephen F. Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_F._Austin) in 1823 and formally constituted in 1835. The Rangers were integral to several important events of Texas history and some of the best-known criminal cases in the history of the [Old West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Old_West).[^cite_note-444]&#x20;

The Texas constitution defines the responsibilities of county governments, which serve as agents of the state. Commissioners court and court judges are elected to serve as the administrative arm. Most cities in the state, those over 5,000 in population, have [home-rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home-rule) governments. The vast majority of these have charters for [council-manager](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council-manager) forms of government, by which voters elect council members, who hire a professional city manager as an operating officer.[^cite_note-445]&#x20;

### Politics

Main article: [Politics of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Texas)

Further information: [Political party strength in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_party_strength_in_Texas)

See also: [Republican Party of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_of_Texas) and [Texas Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Democratic_Party)



[Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson) of Texas, 36th president of the United States



[George W. Bush](3414021.md) of Texas, 43rd president of the United States

Texas consistently votes for the [Republican Party](32070.md). Republicans have won both Senate seats since 1993 and the last time Texas voted for a Democrat presidential candidate was in 1976.[^cite_note-Fox4TexasStreak-446] Republicans have won every statewide election since 1994.[^cite_note-TexasGOPStreak-447]&#x20;

The Democratic Party dominated [Texas politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Texas) from the turn of the 20th century, imposing racial segregation and white supremacy. It held power until after passage in the mid-1960s of national civil rights legislation enforcing constitutional rights of all citizens.[^cite_note-448][^cite_note-449]&#x20;

The state's conservative White voters began to support Republican presidential candidates by the mid-20th century. After this period, they supported Republicans for local and state offices as well, and most Whites became Republican Party members.[^cite_note-450] The party also attracted some minorities, but many have continued to vote for Democratic candidates. The shift to the Republican Party is much-attributed to the fact the Democratic Party became increasingly [liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism_in_the_United_States) during the 20th century, and thus increasingly out-of-touch with the average Texas voter.[^cite_note-HistoryTXvoters-451] As Texas was always a [conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States) state, voters switched to the Republicans, which now more closely reflected their beliefs.[^cite_note-HistoryTXvoters-451][^cite_note-PBSredstate-452] Commentators have also attributed the shift to Republican political consultant [Karl Rove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Rove), who managed numerous political campaigns in Texas in the 1980s and 1990s.[^cite_note-PBSredstate-452] Other stated reasons included court-ordered redistricting and the growing Sun Belt cities' conservatism unlike the liberal tradition of their northeastern counterparts as well as the popularity of Republican party in the suburbs.[^cite_note-Calvert-2017-126]&#x20;

The [2003 Texas redistricting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_Texas_redistricting) of Congressional districts led by Republican [Tom DeLay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_DeLay), was called by *The New York Times* "an extreme case of partisan [gerrymandering](12987.md)".[^cite_note-NYTGerry-453] A group of Democratic legislators, the "Texas Eleven", fled the state in a [quorum](237014.md)-busting effort to prevent the legislature from acting, but was unsuccessful.[^cite_note-454] The state had already redistricted following the 2000 census. Despite these efforts, the legislature passed a map heavily in favor of Republicans, based on 2000 data and ignoring the estimated nearly one million new residents in the state since then. Career attorneys and analysts at the Department of Justice objected to the plan as diluting the votes of African American and Hispanic voters, but political appointees overrode them and approved it.[^cite_note-NYTGerry-453] Legal challenges to the redistricting reached the national [Supreme Court](31737.md) in the case *[League of United Latin American Citizens v. Perry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_United_Latin_American_Citizens_v._Perry)* (2006), but the court ruled in favor of the state (and Republicans).[^cite_note-455]&#x20;
