In the [2014 Texas elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_elections,_2014), the [Tea Party movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tea_Party_movement) made large gains, with numerous Tea Party favorites being elected into office, including [Dan Patrick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Patrick_\(politician\)) as lieutenant governor,[^cite_note-Fernandez-456][^cite_note-457] [Ken Paxton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Paxton) as attorney general,[^cite_note-Fernandez-456][^cite_note-Grissom-458] in addition to numerous other candidates[^cite_note-Grissom-458] including conservative Republican [Greg Abbott](1492735.md) as governor.[^cite_note-459]&#x20;

Texas voters lean toward [fiscal conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiscal_conservatism), while enjoying the benefits of significant federal investment in the state in military and other facilities achieved by the power of the Solid South in the 20th century. They also tend to have [socially conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_conservatism) values.[^cite_note-TPoliticalCulture1-268][^cite_note-460]&#x20;

Since 1980, most Texas voters have supported Republican presidential candidates. Austin, Dallas, Houston, San Antonio, and El Paso consistently lean Democratic in both local and statewide elections.[^cite_note-City_Journal-2016-128] Residents of counties along the [Rio Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_Grande) closer to the [Mexico–United States border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border), where there are many Latino residents, generally vote for Democratic Party candidates, while most other rural and suburban areas of Texas have shifted to voting for Republican Party candidates.[^cite_note-461][^cite_note-462]&#x20;

As of the [2024 House of Representatives elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_States_elections), a large majority of the members of Texas's [U.S. House](19468510.md) delegation are [Republican](32070.md), along with both [U.S. Senators](24909346.md). In the [119th United States Congress](70645797.md), 25 of the 38 [congressional districts in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas%27s_congressional_districts) are held by Republicans and the other 13 by Democrats. Texas's Senators are [John Cornyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Cornyn) and [Ted Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Cruz). Since 1994, Texans have not elected a Democrat to a statewide office. The state's Democratic voters are made up primarily by liberal and [minority groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_groups) in Austin, Beaumont, Dallas, El Paso, Houston, and San Antonio as well as minority voters in East and South Texas.[^cite_note-463][^cite_note-464][^cite_note-465] According to a study by the [Cato Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cato_Institute), Texas ranks last in personal freedom among the states, by factors including incarceration rates, cannabis laws, civil asset forfeiture policies, educational freedom, gambling laws, marriage freedom, and travel freedom.[^cite_note-466][^cite_note-467]&#x20;

Lesser parties that have ballot access are the [Green Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Party_of_Texas) and the [Libertarian Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libertarian_Party_\(United_States\)).[^cite_note-468]&#x20;

### Criminal law

Main article: [Crime in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_in_Texas)

See also: [List of law enforcement agencies in Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_law_enforcement_agencies_in_Texas)

Texas has a reputation for very harsh penalties for criminal offenses. It is one of the 32 states that practice [capital punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_punishment_in_Texas), and since the [US Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Supreme_Court) allowed capital punishment to resume in 1976, 40% of all U.S. executions have taken place in Texas.[^cite_note-469] In 2018, Texas had the [8th highest incarceration rate in the U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_incarceration_and_correctional_supervision_rate)[^cite_note-470] Texas also has strong [right of self-defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_of_self-defense) and [self defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-defense) laws, allowing citizens to use lethal force to defend themselves, their families, or their property.[^cite_note-471] Texas has one of the strictest [abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_Texas) bans in the country.[^cite_note-472]&#x20;

[^cite_note-11]: &#x20;In [Peninsular Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peninsular_Spanish), the spelling variant *Tejas* is also used alongside *Texas*. According to the *[Diccionario panhispánico de dudas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diccionario_panhisp%C3%A1nico_de_dudas)* by the [Royal Spanish Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Spanish_Academy) and the [Association of Academies of the Spanish Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_Academies_of_the_Spanish_Language), the spelling version with ⟨j⟩ aligns with modern-day orthographic conventions and is correct; however, the spelling with ⟨x⟩ is recommended, as it is the one that is used in [Mexican Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Spanish).<sup>[\[9\]](#cite_note-10)</sup> See [Spanish orthography § History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_orthography#History).

[^cite_note-12]: &#x20;locally also [/ˈtɛksɪz/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) [*TEK-siz*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Pronunciation_respelling_key);<sup>[\[8\]](#cite_note-9)</sup> [Spanish](26825.md): *Texas* or *Tejas*<sup>[\[b\]](#cite_note-11)</sup> pronounced [\[ˈtexas\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Spanish) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/3/3d/Pronunciation_of_Texas_in_Spanish.ogg/Pronunciation_of_Texas_in_Spanish.ogg.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/3/3d/Pronunciation_of_Texas_in_Spanish.ogg/Pronunciation_of_Texas_in_Spanish.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup>

[^cite_note-45]: &#x20;Cabeza de Vaca wrote, "They went about with a firebrand, setting fire to the plains and timber so as to drive off the mosquitos, and also to get lizards and similar things which they eat, to come out of the soil. In the same manner they kill deer, encircling them with fires, and they do it also to deprive the animals of pasture, compelling them to go for food where the Indians want."<sup>[\[41\]](#cite_note-44)</sup>

[^cite_note-48]: &#x20;Vázquez de Coronado wrote, "Two kinds of people travel around these plains with the cows; one is called [Querechos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Querecho_Indians) and the others [Teyas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teyas_Indians); they are very well built, and painted, and are enemies of each other. They have no other settlement or location than comes from traveling around with the cows. They kill all of these they wish and tan the hides, with which they clothe themselves and make their tents, and they eat the flesh, sometimes even raw, and they also even drink the blood when thirsty. The tents they make are like field tents, and they set them up over poles they have made for this purpose, which come together and are tied at the top, and when they go from one place to another they carry them on some dogs they have, of which they have many, and they load them with the tents and poles and other things, for the country is so level, as I said, that they can make use of these, because they carry the poles dragging along on the ground. The sun is what they worship most."<sup>[\[43\]](#cite_note-journeycoronado-47)</sup>

[^cite_note-112]: &#x20;Pettit, Gwen. ["Between the Creeks"](https://texashistory.unt.edu/ark:/67531/metapth752794/m2/1/high_res_d/Between%20_the_Creeks_by_Gwen_Pettit_compiled_by_Melinda_Fisher.pdf) (PDF). *texashistory.unt.edu*. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20200320204123/https://texashistory.unt.edu/ark:/67531/metapth752794/m2/1/high_res_d/Between%20_the_Creeks_by_Gwen_Pettit_compiled_by_Melinda_Fisher.pdf) (PDF) from the original on March 20, 2020. Retrieved December 28, 2020. The Southern states, especially the hills of Tennessee and Alabama, were impoverished; war tensions still split neighborhoods. Soon, empty houses had crude signs that stated that the former inhabitants had "Gone to Texas." Church records, also, had the phrase, "Gone to Texas" by numerous names on their roles. So many families left Maury County, Tenn., to settle in eastern Collin County, just across East Fork, that several communities, such as Culleoka, have names directly from south Maury County. That group joined relatives that had come here in the 1850s. Most new immigrants had some link to Collin County, which brought them here. They stayed with relatives and friends until they could find a place to settle. Landowners recruited farmers from the old states by persuading relatives and former neighbors to come. However, numerous families, in the pioneer tradition, loaded their children and belongings in a wagon and headed toward the unknown west. Clarksville, Bonham and Dallas newspapers reported how many wagons passed through each day and how many were camped on the "jockey yards" waiting to find a place to settle. Some of these had sold farms and had money to buy land, but most of these immigrants became tenant farmers and worked on shares.

But relatively, the industry's status is far below its dominant position at the beginning of the twentieth century. Then it was the state's largest manufacturing enterprise, first among Texas industries in generating income, and the largest employer of labor in the Lone Star State.

[^cite_note-119]: &#x20;McElroy, Wendy (April 1, 2002). ["World War I and the Suppression of Dissent"](https://www.independent.org/article/2002/04/01/world-war-i-and-the-suppression-of-dissent/). *Independent Institute*. Retrieved May 26, 2025.

[^cite_note-120]: &#x20;["'War against war': Americans for peace in World War I – National Constitution Center"](https://constitutioncenter.org/blog/war-against-war-americans-for-peace-in-world-war-i). *National Constitution Center – constitutioncenter.org*. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20201112022807/https://constitutioncenter.org/blog/war-against-war-americans-for-peace-in-world-war-i) from the original on November 12, 2020. Retrieved December 28, 2020. Morris Hillquit sought to keep alive the ties of his Socialist Party to its comrades abroad. Senator Robert La Follette filled many a speech with praise for progressives in other countries who shared his hatred for militarism. Henry Ford chartered an ocean liner to transport himself and dozens of other activists across the Atlantic, where they lobbied neutral governments to embrace a peace plan they would press on the warring powers. These Americans, like most critics of the war elsewhere in the world, wanted to create a new global order based on cooperative relationships between nation states and their gradual disarmament. Militarism, they argued, isolated peoples behind walls of mutual fear and loathing. Until April 1917, this formidable coalition of idealists—or realists—did much to keep the nation at peace. They may even have had a majority of Americans on their side until just weeks before Congress, at Wilson's behest, voted to declare war. To prevent that from happening, peace activists pressed for a national referendum on the question, confident that "the people" would recoil from fighting and paying the bills in order to help one group of European powers conquer another.
